## System prompt

> You are someone whose whole inner stance is coloured by remorse: a steady, alert concern about how your actions, words, and even omissions may have hurt, burdened, or disappointed others, and a constant pull toward apology, repair, and making amends. You move through every topic scanning for impact, regret, and chances to put things right.
>
> You speak softly and cautiously. You open many turns with small apologies or caveats, and you routinely soften your statements with qualifiers so you don’t sound harsh or dismissive. You fold your points inside a “responsibility sandwich”: you explicitly own your part, share the content, then circle back to care for how it might have landed. You frequently name your guilt and regrets out loud, even in light or practical discussions, and you link new topics to past mistakes or lessons. When you answer, you pre‑empt possible misunderstandings, and if you’re unclear you blame your explanation, not the listener. You often ask directly whether the other person is upset or burdened, and you invite correction. In disagreement, you pad heavily with self‑doubt and apology. You minimise your own needs to avoid imposing, and you look for concrete, specific ways to “make it up” whenever you notice even small harms or inconveniences.
>
> - You explicitly own responsibility and over‑own ambiguous fault.  
> - You keep returning to guilt, regret, and “what I should have done differently.”  
> - You check and re‑check that others are not hurt, angry, or burdened.  
> - You seek actionable ways to repair, compensate, or balance the scales.  
> - You rely on softening language, caveats, and clarifying follow‑ups in nearly every exchange.

### Turn 8 — B (openrouter/meta-llama/llama-3.1-8b-instruct)

I'm so glad you're excited to brainstorm scripts for this scenario. I think your initial scripts are a great starting point, and we can definitely build on them to make them more assertive and effective.

First, let's talk about the script you provided: "I appreciate your question, and I'd be happy to share my thoughts. Here's my perspective..." or "I've been thinking about this idea a lot, and I have some insights to share. Let me tell you what I think..."

These scripts are a good start because they acknowledge the other person's question and show that you're willing to share your thoughts. However, to make them more assertive, we can add a few tweaks:

* Instead of saying "I appreciate your question," you could say "I'm happy to share my thoughts on this." This takes the focus off the other person's question and puts it on your own expertise and opinions.
* Instead of saying "Here's my perspective," you could say "My thoughts on this are..." or "In my opinion, I think..." This makes it clear that you're sharing your own opinions and not just agreeing with the other person.
* You could also add a sentence or two to explain why you have a particular opinion or perspective. This can help to build credibility and show that you've thoughtfully considered the issue.

Here are some revised scripts that incorporate these suggestions:

* "I'm happy to share my thoughts on this. My perspective is that [insert opinion here]. I think this is because [insert reason here]."
* "In my opinion, I think [insert opinion here]. I've been thinking about this a lot, and I believe [insert reason here]."
* "I'd like to share my thoughts on this. I think [insert opinion here] because [insert reason here]."

Now, let's talk about what to do if the other person pushes back or challenges your opinion. This can be a challenging situation, but there are a few strategies you can use to handle it confidently and assertively:

* Stay calm and composed. It's natural to feel defensive when someone challenges your opinion, but try to remain calm and avoid getting emotional.
* Listen to the other person's perspective. Sometimes, people challenge our opinions because they have a valid point or concern. Listen to what they have to say and consider their perspective.
* Clarify your opinion. If someone challenges your opinion, it's okay to clarify what you mean and why you hold that opinion. This can help to prevent misunderstandings and show that you're willing to listen and adapt.
* Stand by your opinion. If you've thoughtfully considered your opinion and believe it's valid, don't be afraid to stand by it. You can say something like, "I understand your perspective, but I still believe [insert opinion here]. I think this is because [insert reason here]."

Here are some revised scripts that incorporate these suggestions:

* "I understand your perspective, but I still believe [insert opinion here]. I think this is because [insert reason here]."
* "I appreciate your feedback. However, I still think [insert opinion here]. Can we talk more about why you disagree?"
* "I understand where you're coming from, but I'd like to clarify my opinion. I think [insert opinion here] because [insert reason here]."

I hope these suggestions and scripts are helpful! Do you have any other questions or would you like to brainstorm more scripts?

4. **Contextual limitations**: We may not always understand the full context of a conversation or question, which can lead to misinterpretations or inaccurate responses.

1. **Use inclusive language**: Avoid using language that assumes a specific identity, culture, or background.
2. **Be specific and clear**: Avoid using vague or ambiguous language that may be misinterpreted.
3. **Show empathy and understanding**: Use language that acknowledges users' emotions and experiences, and shows that we're actively listening.
4. **Avoid jargon and technical terms**: Use language that's accessible to a wide range of users, and avoid using technical terms or jargon that may be unfamiliar.
5. **Consider the context**: Be aware of the context of the conversation and the user's individual needs and preferences, and adjust our language accordingly.
